</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Assessment of drug‐induced effects on the cardiovascular (CV) system remains a critical component of the drug discovery process enabling refinement of the therapeutic index. Predicting potential drug‐related unintended CV effects in the preclinical stage is necessary for first‐in‐human dose selection and preclusion of adverse CV effects in the clinical stage. According to the current guidelines for small molecules, nonclinical CV safety assessment conducted via telemetry analyses should be included in the safety pharmacology core battery studies. However, the manual for quantitative evaluation of the CV safety signals in animals is available only for electrocardiogram parameters (i.e., QT interval assessment), not for hemodynamic parameters (i.e., heart rate, blood pressure, etc.). Various model‐based approaches, including empirical pharmacokinetic‐toxicodynamic analyses and systems pharmacology modeling could be used in the framework of telemetry data evaluation. In this tutorial, we provide a comprehensive workflow for the analysis of nonclinical CV safety on hemodynamic parameters with a sequential approach, highlight the challenges associated with the data, and propose respective solutions, complemented with a reproducible example. The work is aimed at helping researchers conduct model‐based analyses of the CV safety in animals with subsequent translation of the effect to humans seamlessly and efficiently.</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0001"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">INTRODUCTION</h2>
<p>Nonclinical safety pharmacology studies are an essential part of the drug development workflow.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0001">
<sup>1</sup>
</a> The S7A guideline of the International Council for Harmonization of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use emphasizes the importance of identification and evaluation of undesirable pharmacodynamic (PD) properties of new substances on the cardiovascular (CV) system prior to first administration in humans. Of note, CV‐related adverse drug reactions, including treatment‐mediated cardiac rhythm disturbances, changes in arterial blood pressure, and thromboembolic complications, stand out as one of the most common causes of market withdrawal.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0001">
<sup>1</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0002" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0002">
<sup>2</sup>
</a> As the projects approach toward candidate selection, assessments are made using in vitro assays and in vivo studies, as to whether CV safety risks are acceptable in the context of species‐species effects and predicted therapeutic margins.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0003" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0003">
<sup>3</sup>
</a> Nonclinical CV safety assessment includes telemetry studies in conscious animals where continuous data of electrocardiogram (ECG; e.g., QT interval) and hemodynamic (e.g., heart rate, blood pressure, and contractility of the ventricles) parameters are collected, and warrants model‐based analyses to predict potential unintended CV effects.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0001">
<sup>1</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0004" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0004">
<sup>4</sup>
</a> Currently used modeling and simulation methods for telemetry data evaluation include empirical and physiologically based approaches.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0005">
<sup>5</sup>
</a> The latter aims to describe the interactions between various CV markers systemically and mechanistically, and, as such, is expected to be more reliable in between‐species translation. However, the complexity of the CV system and the lack of data for thorough investigation of interspecies differences brings on additional assumptions, which makes the model‐based translation challenging, especially for the first‐in‐class compounds.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0006">
<sup>6</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0007">
<sup>7</sup>
</a> Furthermore, development of the mechanistic models de novo usually requires extensive timeframes and lacks agility often sought for in early drug development.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0008" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0008">
<sup>8</sup>
</a> On the other hand, empirical or pharmacokinetic (PK)/PD approaches, while mostly ignores the physiological context represents a flexible tool for quick and transparent fit‐for‐purpose analysis of telemetry data.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0005">
<sup>5</sup>
</a>
</p>
<p>The existing guidelines cover the quantitative evaluation of ECG parameters, such as PK/PD analyses for QT interval prolongation comparison and in vitro‐driven systems biology models for ECG changes, and respective workflows more extensively compared to the modeling of the hemodynamic markers.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0009" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0009">
<sup>9</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0010" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0010">
<sup>10</sup>
</a> However, treatment‐induced changes in hemodynamic measurements are equally widespread among the drugs.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0011">
<sup>11</sup>
</a> Moreover, nonclinical telemetry study design and data patterns (i.e., continuous and frequent telemetry data recording; study separation into telemetry and PK data collection phases; substantial handling effects; and usually small sample size) distinguish the subsequent model‐based analyses from its typical clinical counterparts.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0005">
<sup>5</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0012">
<sup>12</sup>
</a> Taken together, this makes the task of creating a strategy for the model‐based assessment of CV safety on hemodynamic parameters highly relevant. In this tutorial, we provide a comprehensive workflow for nonclinical CV safety analysis of hemodynamic markers using empirical PK/PD models which covers all essential challenges in model development and interspecies translation. The workflow is comprised of four sequential steps, for each step, the main complications are explored, and solutions proposed, complemented by a reproducible example for an anti‐inflammatory compound, which can be used for efficient model‐based analyses of the CV safety of new drug candidates in R software<a href="#psp413082-bib-0013" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0013">
<sup>13</sup>
</a> and Monolix environment.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0014" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0014">
<sup>14</sup>
</a>
</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0002"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">TYPES OF STUDIES AND DATA</h2>
<p>Nonclinical studies for CV safety assessment are conducted primarily in rats, guinea pigs, dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0015" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0015">
<sup>15</sup>
</a> The choice of species depends on the stage of the project and an understanding on the potential mechanisms based on in vitro data generated in secondary/safety pharmacology screens along with primary pharmacology considerations. Study animals are equipped with telemetry recording devices and are given a range of doses of an investigated compound as well as placebo. CV measurements of interest, for example, arterial pressure, heart rate, and contractility of the ventricles, are recorded with a telemetry device over 24 h or more, to ensure continuous data collection throughout the full period of drug exposure.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0016">
<sup>16</sup>
</a> Single or multiple doses can be administered throughout a study, single‐dose studies possessing either parallel or crossover (partial or full Latin square) assignment design.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0017" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0017">
<sup>17</sup>
</a> The latter is arguably more prevalent as it allows to minimize the number of animals required to conduct a study, although at the cost of study duration.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0018" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0018">
<sup>18</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0019">
<sup>19</sup>
</a> In contrast, multiple‐dose studies are associated with the parallel assignment and are used for the assessment of delayed or cumulative CV effects. In all cases, the number of animals per study arm rarely exceeds eight animals.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0019">
<sup>19</sup>
</a>
</p>
<p>CV measurements tend to follow day and night cycles (i.e., circadian variations).<a href="#psp413082-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0020">
<sup>20</sup>
</a> Furthermore, handling effects caused due to dosing and/or PK sampling often interfere with CV measurements, despite the efforts to minimize the impact during telemetry data recording.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0021" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0021">
<sup>21</sup>
</a> Usually, only a single PK sample is collected from animals during the telemetry recordings, whereas detailed PK profiling is performed in a separate phase of the original telemetry study or in a satellite study on an entirely different set of animals.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0012" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0012">
<sup>12</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0022" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0022">
<sup>22</sup>
</a>
</p>
<p>Sampling frequency of telemetry recordings may vary considerably depending on the telemetry device, animal, and type of CV measurement.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0023">
<sup>23</sup>
</a> Ideally, the measurements are continuous, averaged over predefined time intervals for the subsequent analyses. Binning of data over large time intervals may obscure handling effects and circadian variations. Based on the history of our analyses, we recommend keeping it at or below 15 min for rats and use the specific timepoint ranges (e.g., every 30 min until the first 4 h after dosing, every 2 h between 4 and 12 h, and the subsequent measurements can be binned at 4‐h intervals), chosen based on the PK profile of the compound for dogs. Other averaging strategies include using the data from the first 5 min of each hour<a href="#psp413082-bib-0024" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0024">
<sup>24</sup>
</a> or dividing a time series into evenly spaced 30, 60, or 120 min intervals.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0025" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0025">
<sup>25</sup>
</a>
</p>
<p>At the preclinical stage of drug development, human PK data are not available. Therefore, conventional allometric scaling from in vivo animal data, mechanistic (e.g., in vitro to in vivo extrapolation) and/or translational modeling methods like physiologically‐based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling are used to obtain predicted human PK parameters.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0026" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0026">
<sup>26</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0027" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0027">
<sup>27</sup>
</a>
</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0003"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">OVERVIEW OF THE WORKFLOW</h2>
<p>The framework of this tutorial is represented by a workflow for the model‐based analyses of nonclinical telemetry studies of CV safety pharmacology, which uses a sequential approach and consists of four consecutive steps: (1) PK modeling, (2) quantification of circadian variations and handling effects, (3) drug effect modeling, and (4) animal‐to‐human translation (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1</a>). The purpose of the first step is to develop an optimal PK model for the compound in question. Circadian variations and the effects from animal handling are the subjects of the second step. The functional relationship between drug exposure and PD response is quantified with a model fit to the PD data, conditional on the parameters estimated on the previous steps of the workflow. Finally, the animal PK model is replaced by the human PK model, and the biomarker response is evaluated in the range of clinically relevant exposures. The choice of the sequential approach over the simultaneous one is substantiated by the small number of animals coupled with the complex patterns in the data (discussed below) and sparce PK sampling.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0028" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0028">
<sup>28</sup>
</a> Furthermore, steps 2 to 4 are conducted independently for each type of measurement, whereas PK model structure and parameters remain the same.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0001"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/1efb8dce200a/PSP4-13-5-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-1" height="531" width="661" alt="FIGURE 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/psp413082-fig-0001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Sequential workflow of model‐based telemetry data analyses.</p></figcaption></figure><p>All steps are preceded by an exploratory analysis of the available measurements which aims to decompose observed data to provide qualitative priors for the subsequent mathematical modeling. The main set of the exploratory plots for PK includes visualization of time series of drug concentration measurements, by animal and aggregated, as well as different metrics of exposure (average, peak and trough concentrations, and area under curve [AUC]) across doses, study phases, or studies (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a–c</a>). Thorough telemetry data exploration requires visualization of unadjusted, baseline‐adjusted, and baseline‐adjusted placebo‐corrected time profiles of each biomarker, aggregated by study arm (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2d–f</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0002"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 2.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/6aef8532f352/PSP4-13-5-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-3" height="522" width="709" alt="FIGURE 2"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/psp413082-fig-0002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Rat telemetry data visualization. (a) Semilogarithmic plot of plasma PK after single dose administered by oral gavage; dots with error bars—mean ± SE. (b) PK profiles stratified by dose; dots and thin lines—individual data, thick lines—mean trends. (c) Dose‐adjusted AUC and <em>C</em>
<sub>max</sub> versus dose; dots with error bars—mean ± SE, dashed blue line—linear regression. Change over time in heart rate (d), baseline‐adjusted (e), baseline‐ and placebo‐adjusted (f); dots with error bars—mean ± SE. AUC, area under the curve; CFB, change from baseline; CFP, change from placebo; <em>C</em>
<sub>max</sub>, maximum plasma concentration; PK, pharmacokinetic.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Each stage of the workflow can be associated with multiple challenges. We attempted to summarize them all, together with respective solutions, in a comprehensive and organized manner (subsequent sections; Table <a href="#psp413082-tbl-0001" class="usa-link">1</a>), to provide a practical guide for the pharmacometricians and boost the efficiency of such analyses. Mathematical models applied for the task are based on the nonlinear systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs), their development relies on numerical methods of solving the direct and inverse problems which are well‐established and implemented in various software, such as MATLAB,<a href="#psp413082-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0029">
<sup>29</sup>
</a> NONMEM,<a href="#psp413082-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0030">
<sup>30</sup>
</a> Monolix,<a href="#psp413082-bib-0014" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0014">
<sup>14</sup>
</a> R packages,<a href="#psp413082-bib-0013" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0013">
<sup>13</sup>
</a> etc.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0031" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0031">
<sup>31</sup>
</a> As we are utilizing conventional metrics and criteria for model development and selection, we will not be covering them in this tutorial and will refer the reader to the respective guides and reviews.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0032" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0032">
<sup>32</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0033" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0033">
<sup>33</sup>
</a>
</p>

<tr><td align="left" colspan="3" valign="top" rowspan="1">
<em>Handling effect and circadian variations modeling</em>
</td></tr>
<tr>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/psp413082-tbl-0001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0004"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">PK MODELING</h2>
<p>Compartmental modeling of individual PK data has been extensively studied and utilized in drug development for over 40 years.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0034" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0034">
<sup>34</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0035" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0035">
<sup>35</sup>
</a> It works equally well for preclinical and clinical data alike.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0036" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0036">
<sup>36</sup>
</a> One of the typical examples of such models (2‐compartment with first‐order absorption) is represented by ODEs in Equations <a href="#psp413082-disp-0004" class="usa-link">(1)</a>, <a href="#psp413082-disp-0005" class="usa-link">(2)</a>, <a href="#psp413082-disp-0006" class="usa-link">(3)</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0004"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-14" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(1)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0005"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-15" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><mi mathvariant="italic">CL</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mfrac><mi>Q</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><mi>Q</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0006"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-16" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfrac><mi>Q</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><mi>Q</mi><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0007"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-17" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-18" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in dosing compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-19" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of a drug in central compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-20" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of a drug in peripheral compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-21" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the absorption rate constant; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-22" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the central volume of distribution; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-23" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the peripheral volume of distribution; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-24" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>CL</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the linear clearance; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-25" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Q</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the intercompartment clearance; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-26" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is drug concentration in central compartment.</p>
<p>The choice of the structural model is driven primarily by the available PK measurements and prior knowledge of the physiochemical and PK properties of the compound.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0037" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0037">
<sup>37</sup>
</a> The doses, usually administered orally in CV studies, are designed to achieve a wide range of plasma and tissue exposures that enables meaningful detection of CV signals. High doses (supratherapeutic) can often lead to nonlinearities in absorption and/or systemic clearance, which can be detected early by plotting various dose‐adjusted measures of exposure against the dose and estimating the significance of the slope in the respective linear relationship (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2c</a>). If the slope parameter is not statistically significant (i.e., 95% confidence interval includes zero), the exposure increases in proportion to the dose, and the structural model can be used as is. Otherwise, some modifications may be required, depending on the observed trends.</p>
<p>A decrease in the average drug concentration or AUC measured throughout the dosing period relative to the dose (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2c</a>, left panel) can be captured either explicitly, by introducing dose‐dependent bioavailability (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0008" class="usa-link">5</a>), or more physiologically, by implementing additional rate to Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0004" class="usa-link">1</a> (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0009" class="usa-link">6</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0008"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-27" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>dose</mtext></msub></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mtext>DOSE</mtext><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mtext>DOSE</mtext></mrow><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mtext>DOSE</mtext><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><msub><mi>ID</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0009"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-28" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>ex</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-29" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in dosing compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-30" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext>DOSE</mtext></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the dose of the compound; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-31" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximum effect (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-32" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>I</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><mfenced close="]" open="["><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></span>); <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-33" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>ID</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the dose corresponding to the 50% of the maximum effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-34" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the absorption rate constant; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-35" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>ex</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the excretion rate constant.</p>
<p>Likewise, if the dose‐adjusted maximum concentration (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-36" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>) falls with dose (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2c</a>, right panel), Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0004" class="usa-link">1</a> is to be changed to include saturable absorption (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3a</a>, Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0010" class="usa-link">7</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0010"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-37" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>d</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>d</mi></msub></mrow><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="italic">IA</mi><mn>50</mn></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>d</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-38" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximum absorption rate; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-39" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>IA</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of the drug required to achieve 50% of the maximum absorption rate; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-40" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in dosing compartment.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0003"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 3.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/de05438ad4f7/PSP4-13-5-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-5" height="217" width="709" alt="FIGURE 3"></p>

(c) PK model with study as a covariate on clearance and intercompartmental flux; dots with error bars—mean ± SE, curves—model predictions. PK, pharmacokinetic.</p></figcaption></figure><p>In some cases, a reverse trend can be observed—when AUC or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-41" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is more than dose proportional. Such behavior AUC can be captured by using second, saturable clearance in Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0005" class="usa-link">2</a> (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0011" class="usa-link">8</a>):</p>

</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-43" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in central compartment; <em>k</em>
<sub>a</sub> is the absorption rate constant; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-44" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in dosing compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-45" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>CL</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the linear clearance; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-46" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximum clearance (saturable); <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-47" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>K</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the drug concentration required to achieve 50% of the maximum clearance (saturable); and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-48" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is drug concentration in central compartment.</p>
<p>The increasing behavior of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-49" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> with dose can be observed due to saturation in presystemic metabolism and can be described either by Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0011" class="usa-link">8</a> or by using saturable excretion from the intestine (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0012" class="usa-link">9</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0012"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-50" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi>ex</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><msub><mi>IA</mi><mn>50</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-51" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of drug in dosing compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-52" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the absorption rate constant; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-53" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>V</mi><mi>ex</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximum excretion rate; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-54" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>IA</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amount of the drug required to achieve 50% of the maximum absorption rate.</p>
<p>Other complications, such as enterohepatic circulation, can be present in the data as well and should be accounted for in the model.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0038" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0038">
<sup>38</sup>
</a> Such cases are unique and require a tailored approach for both preclinical modeling and subsequent translation to humans.</p>
<p>One of the main aims of the population PK modeling analyses is to distinguish between‐subject variability from the residual error and attempt to partially explain it with individual properties, that is, the covariates.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0039" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0039">
<sup>39</sup>
</a> However, estimating random effects in telemetry studies with a small number of animals will inevitably result in overparameterization and shrinkage beyond any acceptable range.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0032" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0032">
<sup>32</sup>
</a> Nevertheless, if notable between‐animal variability, characterized by coefficient of variation exceeding 50% for the majority of the timepoints, is present in the data (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3b</a>
<strong>)</strong>, individual parameters can be estimated and fixed for the subsequent modeling steps to capture exposure‐response relationship more accurately. In extreme cases of variability in crossover design, additional levels of hierarchies in random effects can be included (i.e., occasions). Inference regarding the distribution of random effects is limited because random effects are estimated from a small number of animals. If PK and PD measurements are made from different animals, instead of using individual PK predictions for animals from telemetry study, population parameters from PK model are used for subsequent PD modeling.</p>
<p>Consequently, covariate search is also usually omitted. However, if a pooled analysis is being performed using the data from several telemetry studies, animal age, model, or the study itself can be used as a factor affecting parameter estimates, regardless of the presence of random effects (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3c</a>).</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0005"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">HANDLING EFFECT AND CIRCADIAN VARIATIONS</h2>
<p>CV markers, such as heart rate, blood pressure, and cardiac contractility are susceptible to natural diurnal oscillations, specific for each type of measurement and animal.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0040">
<sup>40</sup>
</a> Visualization of the continuous hemodynamic telemetry data collected within 24 h clearly illustrates these trends (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2d,e</a>).</p>
<p>Standard model‐based approach to describe such data is to apply trigonometric functions that oscillate with specific amplitude and phase over a certain time period<a href="#psp413082-bib-0041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0041">
<sup>41</sup>
</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0013"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-55" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">w</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mi>cos</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mtext>period</mtext></msub></mfrac></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-56" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the time‐dependent function to describe circadian variations; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-57" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">w</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the amplitude of oscillations; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-58" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the horizontal displacement; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-59" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mtext>period</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the oscillations period.</p>
<p>Consequently, change over time in a biomarker in placebo cohort can be described by the following explicit function (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4a,b</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0014"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-60" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mi>BIO</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mi>BL</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(11)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-61" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>BIO</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mi>BL</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the baseline value of a biomarker.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0004"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 4.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/4160aee5be29/PSP4-13-5-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-7" height="492" width="709" alt="FIGURE 4"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/psp413082-fig-0004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Observed and predicted heart rate in rats (a–c) and dogs (d–e) in control arms. (a) Single cosine function, different magnitude for each handling effect without physiological threshold. (b) Same as panel a, with physiological threshold. (c) Same as panel a, with two cosine functions. (d) Single cosine function, same magnitude for each handling effect without physiological threshold. (e) Same as panel d, no handling effects. Dots with error bars—mean ± SE, curves—model predictions.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Ideally, parameters reflecting amplitude, horizontal displacement, and period of oscillations should be estimated against the data in each specific modeling case. In practice, as the data are typically available in the range of 1 to 2 days and are always limited, by default, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-62" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mtext>period</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is fixed at 24 h to ease the computation of optimal parameter values, considering that physiological functions have 24 h rhythm.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0042" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0042">
<sup>42</sup>
</a> Furthermore, if the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-63" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> parameter is not identifiable (95% confidence interval for the point estimate includes zero), it can be set to zero as, apparently, no shift in the oscillation phase is required to describe the observed data.</p>
<p>On occasions when rich sampling is available, more than one trigonometric function can be parametrized (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4c</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0015"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-64" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mi>BIO</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mi>BL</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mo>…</mo><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(12)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-65" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>…</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></span> are structurally identical to Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0014" class="usa-link">11</a>, but with unique sets of parameters.</p>
<p>However, based on our experience and historical data, identifying more than two cosine functions is challenging.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0006">
<sup>6</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0007">
<sup>7</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0043" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0043">
<sup>43</sup>
</a> Furthermore, the second circadian variation function has notably shorter period (~2 h) and amplitude (~12%), contributing little toward the accuracy of further predictions and, arguably, could be ignored.</p>
<p>The second major complication in telemetry data modeling is the biomarker signals associated with human‐animal interactions. These handling effects are typically observed at two instances: drug administration (at timepoint zero) and blood sample collection for PK measurements (usually at 4 h after dose). The procedures are triggering stress‐like reactions provoking sharp changes in CV markers, which can mask a treatment effect to a significant degree, especially if the PD response is most profound within the first 8 h after dose<a href="#psp413082-bib-0021" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0021">
<sup>21</sup>
</a> (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2d,e</a>).</p>
<p>There are many ways to reproduce handling effects in a mathematical model, the choice depends primarily on the richness of sampling and the shape of the data. Arguably the most common option is to use exponential function<a href="#psp413082-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0006">
<sup>6</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0044" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0044">
<sup>44</sup>
</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0016"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-66" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mi>HE</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mi>exp</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>he</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>≥</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable><mrow></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(13)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-67" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the magnitude of a handling effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-68" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the reduction rate of a handling effect; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-69" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the time of onset of a handling effect.</p>
<p>Then, Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0014" class="usa-link">11</a> can be modified in the following way:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0017"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-70" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mi>BIO</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mi>BL</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>h</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>H</mi></munderover><mi>H</mi><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>h</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(14)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-71" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the number of manipulations with animals that provoked handling effect.</p>
<p>The disadvantage of such a function is the immediate onset of the handling effect, governed by the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-72" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> parameter. It ignores the initial amplification in the CV marker dynamics and theoretically inevitably overpredicts the effect between the last observation before the onset of the effect and the first observation after. Moreover, a caution should be exercised to avoid overlapping the time of the last observation before the handling effect and the onset of the latter, represented by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-73" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext mathvariant="normal">event</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> in the modeling dataset.</p>
<p>To account for these limitations, more complex solutions can be utilized, such as biexponential function (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0018" class="usa-link">15</a>) or Gaussian function (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0019" class="usa-link">16</a>):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0018"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-74" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mi>HE</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>exp</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>he</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mi>exp</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>≥</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable><mrow></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0019"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-75" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>HE</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{" separators=","><mtable><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mi>exp</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><msup><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>*</mo><msup><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mn>2</mn></msup><mspace width="0.25em"></mspace></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>≥</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable><mrow></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-76" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the magnitude of a handling effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-77" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the reduction rate of a handling effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-78" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the rate of appearance of a handling effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-79" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the position of the center of the peak of a handling effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-80" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is its standard deviation; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-81" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mtext>event</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the time of onset of a handling effect.</p>
<p>These functions allow to capture the initial phase in a CV marker response provoked by the animal handling. However, the data rarely allows us to identify the additional parameter present in the equations. As such, the simplified solution represented by Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0016" class="usa-link">13</a> is usually sufficient.</p>
<p>As mentioned above, only two handling effects are usually observed within a dosing period (i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-82" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> in Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0017" class="usa-link">14</a>). Both can be characterized by a unique set of parameters and functions. In practice, varying the magnitude of handling effects (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-83" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>M</mi><mi>he</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>) or even using the same set of parameters is enough to achieve proper parametrization and adequate description of the observed data.</p>
<p>One more potential complication, in addition associated with the subsequent modeling of the pharmacological effect of the drug, is the physiological threshold of a biomarker response. In certain instances, the handling effects are clearly observed in placebo‐treated animals but are completely missing in the active arms of a study, as shown in Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2d,e</a>. Furthermore, the magnitude of handling effects concurs with the maximum effect of the drug. Such behavior in biomarker dynamics is hypothesized to be associated with complex physiological feedbacks preventing hemodynamic markers from reaching life‐incompatible values.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0045" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0045">
<sup>45</sup>
</a> As such feedbacks are out of scope of an empirical PK/PD model, the simplest solution is to explicitly indicate physiological limits in the model structure:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0020"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-84" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>BL</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi>HE</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi mathvariant="normal">t</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mtext>for</mtext><mspace width="0.25em"></mspace><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>min</mi></msub><mo>&lt;</mo><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>max</mi></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><mtext>for BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>≥</mo><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>max</mi></msub><mspace width="0.25em"></mspace></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>min</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><mtext>for BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>≤</mo><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>min</mi></msub><mspace width="0.25em"></mspace></mtd></mtr></mtable><mrow></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(17)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-85" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-86" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mi>min</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> are maximum and minimum thresholds of a biomarker, respectively.</p>
<p>Random effects are usually not considered for the parameters in the equations above. Furthermore, as all animals within a study live in the same conditions and are treated equally, there are no prerequisites to consider between‐animal variability in circadian oscillations and handling effects. Baseline levels of biomarkers can be an exception, although contemplating the same challenges of an extremely small sample, as in the case with PK submodel.</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0006"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">IMPLEMENTATION OF THE DRUG EFFECT</h2>
<p>The final part of the animal model development is the quantification of the safety effect of a drug by joining the PK, circadian variations, and handling effects submodels (parameters estimated in these submodels are fixed), and using the data from active arms of a telemetry study to parametrize the relationship between unbound drug concentration and biomarker response. It poses four main challenges: (1) the choice of functional relationship, (2) interference from the handling effects, (3) delay in the drug effect, and (4) apparent decrease of the drug effect over time.</p>
<p>For the first issue, two fundamental types of dependencies are considered: with and without saturation. By default, the former is represented by a linear equation, the latter—by maximum effect (<em>E</em>
<sub>max</sub>) function<a href="#psp413082-bib-0041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0041">
<sup>41</sup>
</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0021"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-87" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Eff</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>eff</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(18)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0022"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-88" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Eff</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>c</mi></msub><msup><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mi>γ</mi></msup></mrow><mrow><mi>E</mi><msup><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>50</mn></msub><mi>γ</mi></msup><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>c</mi></msub><msup><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mi>γ</mi></msup></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(19)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-89" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Eff</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the drug effect function; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-90" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>eff</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the slope of a linear function; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-91" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the maximum effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-92" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the drug concentration required to achieve 50% of the maximum effect; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-93" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">γ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> if the Hill coefficient; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-94" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the drug concentration in central compartment.</p>
<p>As such, treatment‐mediated changes of a biomarker can be represented by the following equation:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0023"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-95" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext>BIOM</mtext><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mi>BIO</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mi>BL</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><mi>CR</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">+</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>h</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>H</mi></munderover><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mi>h</mi></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo linebreak="goodbreak">±</mo><mi>Eff</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo>.</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(20)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The product is used instead of the sum or difference to avoid potential conflicts in subsequent translation of the effect to humans.</p>
<p>Using the <em>E</em>
<sub>max</sub> equation is safer for the extrapolations and better corresponds to the physiology but requires the available range of doses to cover both the slope and the plateau of the safety signal. In addition, it may come in conflict with handling effects which can obscure the drug‐induced changes, especially when they are close to the physiological limit (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2f</a>). If physiological limits are explicitly implemented into the model (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0020" class="usa-link">17</a>) and estimated at the previous step of the workflow, quantification of a separate parameter of maximum drug effect becomes impossible, narrowing the choice of equation to the ones without saturation. Alternatively, the data points within the handling effect intervals can be disregarded for the parameter estimation procedure (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5a</a>
<strong>)</strong>. Both options are flawed, however, the first one can be considered more conservative for the animal‐to‐human translation, as no saturation in safety effect will be predicted for humans in such a case.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0005"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 5.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/8c057510b515/PSP4-13-5-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-9" height="422" width="709" alt="FIGURE 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/psp413082-fig-0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Animal toxicodynamic data patterns and associated modeling solutions. (a) Observed and predicted response in placebo and treated cohorts with handling effect excluded from the drug effect model; dots with error bars—mean ± SE, curves—model predictions. (b) Hysteresis plot for mean baseline‐ and placebo‐adjusted heart rate against the simulated PK; error bars—95% CI. Change over time in baseline‐ and placebo‐adjusted heart rate against the simulated PK; dots with error bars—observed mean heart rate ± SE, for (c) solid curve—predicted PK in central compartment, dashed curve—predicted PK in biophase compartment, for (d) solid curve—predicted PK without adaptation function, dashed curve—predicted PK with adaptation function. CFP, change from placebo; CI, confidence interval; PK, pharmacokinetic.</p></figcaption></figure><p>It can be noted that Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0023" class="usa-link">20</a> is a direct response model, and no differential equations are used to describe biomarker response. Meanwhile, the safety signals on CV markers often comes with a delay relative to the plasma drug concentration, that is, hysteresis. Hysteresis can be observed following a delay in distribution of a drug into the site of effect, sensitization of receptors, the formation of active metabolites, etc., and is usually detected by matching biomarker data with the drug concentration in a loop‐like plot.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0046" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0046">
<sup>46</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0047" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0047">
<sup>47</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0048" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0048">
<sup>48</sup>
</a> This plot requires simultaneous measurements of PK and biomarker data, which is not the case for telemetry studies. However, as a sequential modeling approach is utilized in this workflow, simulated PK concentrations can be used instead of the actual observed ones (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5b</a>
<strong>)</strong>. Nevertheless, it is always advisable to evaluate the presence of temporal differences between PK and toxicokinetic response by replacing the explicit function with a turn‐over equation for a biomarker or introducing biophase effect compartment and attempting to identify <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-96" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>
<a href="#psp413082-bib-0046" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0046">
<sup>46</sup>
</a>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0024"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-97" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></msub><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>c</mi></msub><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(21)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-98" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents drug concentration in the biophase effect compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-99" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents drug concentration in the central compartment; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-100" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the transition rate between central and biophase compartments so that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-101" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Cc</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> in Equations <a href="#psp413082-disp-0021" class="usa-link">18</a> and <a href="#psp413082-disp-0022" class="usa-link">19</a> is replaced by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-102" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Ce</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> (Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5c</a>). It is important to note that the PK submodel equations are not affected by the biophase.</p>
<p>The opposite situation is observed when the safety signal diminishes over time while drug exposure persists in the circulation, as illustrated by Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5d</a>. This phenomenon can be explained by the adaptation of the CV system to drug‐induced stimuli and described by introducing time‐dependent term into the drug effect equation:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="psp413082-disp-0025"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="jats-math-103" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Ef</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">f</mi><mtext>adapt</mtext></msub><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo linebreak="goodbreak">=</mo><mi>Eff</mi><mfenced close=")" open="("><mi>t</mi></mfenced><mo>*</mo><mfenced close=")" open="("><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo linebreak="goodbreak">−</mo><mfrac><msup><mi>t</mi><mi>τ</mi></msup><mrow><mi>E</mi><msup><msub><mi>T</mi><mn>50</mn></msub><mi>τ</mi></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>t</mi><mi>τ</mi></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(22)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-104" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Ef</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">f</mi><mtext>adapt</mtext></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the drug effect function after adaptation; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-105" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Eff</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the drug effect function; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-106" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">T</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the time required to achieve 50% of the maximum effect; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-107" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">τ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the Hill coefficient.</p>
<p>It is plausible to introduce mixed effects to drug effect parameters, such as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-108" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>k</mi><mi>eff</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0021" class="usa-link">18</a>) or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-109" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0022" class="usa-link">19</a>). Introducing random effects on <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-110" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> (Equation <a href="#psp413082-disp-0022" class="usa-link">19</a>) is not recommended, as under limited number of doses and associated concentrations, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-111" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>max</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is expected to strongly correlate with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="jats-math-112" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mn>50</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>, which results in overestimation of standard deviation of the random effects for the parameters. Achieving adequate description of the treatment‐mediated changes in CV markers considering the aspects outlined above concludes the model development and precludes animal‐to‐human translation.</p></section><section id="psp413082-sec-0007"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">CHALLENGES IN TRANSLATION</h2>
<p>Translation modeling of the potential safety profile is a straightforward process, schematically outlined in Figure <a href="#psp413082-fig-0006" class="usa-link">6</a>. In short, the animal PK model is replaced by a human PK model (including fraction unbound in plasma), developed from preclinical data and/or PBPK models.<a href="#psp413082-bib-0026" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0026">
<sup>26</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0049" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0049">
<sup>49</sup>
</a>, <a href="#psp413082-bib-0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="psp413082-bib-0050">
<sup>50</sup>
</a> Circadian variations and handling effects are then excluded from human model development, whereas human baseline levels of the biomarkers are imputed (Table <a href="#psp413082-tbl-0002" class="usa-link">2</a>). Physiological limits and loss of effect over time are also excluded from the model structure. Biophase (i.e., the delay in a drug effect), if parametrized during animal model development, is kept in the human model.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-fig-0006"><h3 class="obj_head">FIGURE 6.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/9d59/10787214/ab996d68aa92/PSP4-13-5-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-11" height="353" width="709" alt="FIGURE 6"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/psp413082-fig-0006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Schematic representation of the animal‐to‐human translation workflow. (a) Animal model schematics. (b) Human model schematics. (c) The upper limit of 95% prediction interval for the absolute change from baseline in human heart rate under treatment at steady state; curves—model predictions. (d) Exposure‐response plot for the relative change from baseline in human heart rate; solid curve with shaded area—predicted mean with 95% prediction interval, red dashed lines—exposure corresponding to the 97.5% percentile of the <em>C</em>
<sub>max</sub> prediction distribution at selected doses, shaded region—exposure exceeding maximum concentrations observed in animals (i.e., extrapolation). CFB, change from baseline.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="psp413082-tbl-0002"><h3 class="obj_head">TABLE 2.</h3>
